L1523: ## 19.4.2. Population PK Analysis
L1524: ## 19.4.2.1. Executive Summary
L1525: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1526: The Applicant submitted a population PK report entitled "Population PK Analysis of Inavolisib in Patients with Locally Advanced or Metastatic PIK3CA-Mutant Hormone-Receptor Positive Breast Cancer" to characterize the pharmacokinetics of inavolisib after oral administration in patients from studies GO39374 and WO41554. The final population PK model was used to estimate the PK parameters and exposure of inavolisib as a single agent or in combination with other agents in patients with locally advanced or metastatic PIK3CA-mutated solid tumors and in patients with locally advanced or metastatic, PIK3CA-mutated breast cancer. The potential effect of the intrinsic and extrinsic factors on inavolisib were also assessed.
L1527: The population PK model for inavolisib was developed based on PK observations from a total of 336 subjects, with 187 patients from Study GO39374 and 149 patients from Study WO41554. The final population PK model of inavolisib was a three-compartment model with first-order absorption, linear elimination, and a mixed proportional and additive error model. Effects of eGFR on CL, body weight and race on Vc and formulation on Ka were included in the final model. The typical values of CL and V of inavolisib were 8.83 L/h and 155 L, respectively.
L1528: eGFR effect on CL, weight and race (Asian) effects on Vc were identified as significant covariates, with lower CL for subjects with lower eGFR. The rate of absorption was found to be slower with the phase 1 formulation and the Phase 1 resupply formulation compared to the phase 3 formulation. No impact from combination therapy (fulvestrant, palbociclib, letrozole) was identified.
L1529: Patients with moderate renal impairment (N=27) showed higher exposure than patients with normal renal function (N=185) (GMR of steady-state AUC_{0-\infty}: 1.42). Patients with mild renal impairment (N=124) and normal renal function had comparable exposure. No impact on inavolisib exposure was observed for patients with mild hepatic impairment (N=111) vs normal hepatic function (N=224).
L1530: In general, the Applicant's population PK analysis is considered acceptable for the purpose of estimating the PK and exposure parameters of inavolisib in adult patients with locally advanced
L1531: or metastatic, PIK3CA-mutated breast cancer. However, no information is available in the population PK analysis to assess the effect of moderate to severe hepatic impairment and severe renal impairment on inavolisib PK, and information to assess the effect of moderate renal impairment on inavolisib PK is too limited to draw a definitive conclusion. Therefore, dedicated studies to evaluate these effects are warranted. Refer to Section 6.1.2.2 for results of the moderate renal impairment group in the ongoing renal impairment study.
L1532: The Applicant's analyses were verified by the reviewer, with no significant discordance identified. The results of the population PK analyses were used to support the proposed labeling statements regarding PK parameters of inavolisib and the effects of intrinsic and extrinsic factors.
L1533: ## 19.4.2.2. PPK Assessment Summary
L1534: **The Applicant's Position:** PopPK analysis was performed using 3863 samples from 336 patients (187 from Study GO39374 and 149 from Study WO41554). The final model was a three-compartment model with first-order rate of absorption and linear elimination, a combined additive and proportional error model, a block omega on CL and Vc and between-subject variability (BSV) on Ka, and Vp.
L1535: The typical values of CL and Vc of inavolisib were 8.83 L/h and 155 L, respectively. For the peripheral compartments, the typical values of Q, Q2, Vp and Vp2 were 7.50 L/h, 0.14 L/h, 68 L, and 31 L, respectively. The rate of absorption was estimated at 0.92 h-1 for Phase 3 formulation. The population PK model included a significant effect of eGFR on CL, with an exponent of 0.441 [i.e., (eGFR/86)0.441]. This suggests a higher clearance for subjects with higher eGFR. Body weight was significant on Vc, with an exponent of 0.635 [i.e., (WT/65)0.635]. Subjects with a higher body weight had therefore a higher volume of distribution. The volume of distribution was also found to be approximately 25% lower in Asian subjects compared to non-Asian subjects. The rate of absorption was found to be approx. 51% slower with the Phase 1 formulation and phase 1 resupply formulation compared to the phase 3 formulation. No apparent trend remaining between random effects of parameters and the covariates were seen in the final model.
L1536: Based on the goodness-of-fit plots and prediction-corrected visual predictive checks, the popPK model was considered adequate to derive the patient-level exposure metrics (AUC, Cmax, Cmin) for subsequent exposure-response analyses. The exposure metrics were also used to compare exposures between the sub-groups of interests by simulations.
L1537: The sub-groups of interests included race (Asian vs. non-Asian), patients enrolled from China sites (Chinese vs. Non-Chinese), hepatic impairment status (mild vs. normal), renal impairment status (mild or moderate vs. normal), age group (\ge 65 years vs. &lt; 65 years; 65 - &lt; 75 years, or \ge 75
L1538: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219249}
L1539: {Inavolisib}
L1540: years vs. &lt;65 years), body weight group (tertile of body weight) and formulation status (Phase 1/ Phase 1 resupply vs. Phase 3). At steady-state, most of the GMR of exposure metrics between sub-groups of interest were within 0.8 and 1.25, with the exception of the following sub-groups: moderate renal impairment vs. normal renal function (GMR of 1.42, 1.35 and 1.56 for AUC, Cmax, and Cmin, respectively); Phase 1 / Phase 1 resupply formulations vs. Phase 3 formulation (GMR of 0.79 and 0.75 for Cmax, GMR of 1.33 and 1.16 for Cmin); age ≥75 years vs. age &lt;65 years (GMR of 1.38 for Cmin), based on only 13 patients with age ≥ 75 years, with GMR for AUC and Cmax remaining within 0.8 to 1.25. The impact of most of those variables are less pronounced on AUC which reflects the entire inavolisib exposure.
L1541: General Information
Objectives of PPK Analysis	To identify potential covariates effects that may explain the variability in PK parameters. To provide a quantitative assessment of the potential effect of the intrinsic and extrinsic factors on inavolisib PK. To provide post hoc predictions from the PK model that would be used to perform E-R analysis.
Study Included	Phase I Study GO39374; Phase III Study WO41554 (INAVO120)
Dose(s) Included	Study GO39374 was a first-in-human, Phase I, open-label, dose-escalation study conducted from 3 to 12 mg (3, 6, 9, 12mg); Study WO41554 (INAVO120) was a Phase III, randomized, double-blind, placebo controlled, multicenter study conducted at 9mg.
Population Included	Patients with PIK3CA-mutant HR+ LA/mBC
Population Characteristics (Table 3, Table 4)	General	The median (range) age in the PK population dataset was 56.6 years (27.0 to 85.0 years), with 25.6% >= 65 years and 3.9% >=75 years. The median (range) body weight was 65.0 kg (39.0 – 159 kg). Most subjects were White (i.e., 67% White and 18.5% Asian) and female (98%).
Organ Impairment	Hepatic impairment (NCI): normal 224 (66.7%); Mild group-1 107 (31.8%); Mild group-2 4 (1.2%)
Renal impairment (CrCL): normal 185 (55.1%); Mild 124 (36.9%); Moderate 27 (8.0%)
Pediatrics (if any)	No pediatric patients were included.
No. of Patients, PK Samples, and BLQ	The PK analysis dataset included 4364 PK observations from 346 subjects. After exclusion of Arm G from study GO39374 (48 PK records) and the flagged PK record (107 records), a total of 4209 PK observations from 338 subjects were available. Of these, 3863 (91.8%) from 336 subjects were quantifiable concentrations, and 346 (8.2%) records were BLQ observations. Only 63 BLQ records were observed after administration of the first dose ("post-dose BLQ"), accounting for less than 1.5% of all observations. All the rest of BLQ records (283 records) were "pre-dose BLQ". Hence, exclusion of BLQ samples from the analysis was deemed acceptable.
Sampling Schedule	Rich Sampling	For WO41554, in a subset of patients from China (n=14), intensive PK sampling was collected (0.5, 1, 2, 3, 8, 24hr at cycle 1 day1 and cycle 1 day 15). For GO39374, in all arms except arm E and F, the intensive sampling was collected at slightly different timepoints
In ITT Population	For WO41554, sparse samplings were collected at cycle 1 day1 (Predose, 3 hr post-dose), cycle 1 day8 (Predose), cycle 1 day15 (Predose, 3 hr post-dose) and cycle 2day15 (Predose). For GO39374, sparse samplings were collected at cycle 1 day1 (Predose, 4 and 24hr post-dose) for arm E and cycle 1 day15 (Predose, 24 hr post-dose) for arm E and F.
Covariates Evaluated	Static	Age, body weight, body mass index, body surface area, sex, race, albumin, alanine transaminase, aspartate transaminase, bilirubin, eGFR, CrCL, renal impairments status, hepatic impairment status, ECOG, tumor burden, presence of liver metastasis, platelets count, neutrophils, lymphocytes, total protein
Time-varying	Food, formulation
Final Model	Summary	Acceptability [FDA's comments]
Software and Version	The population PK analysis was performed using non-linear mixed effect model (NLME) in NONMEM (Version 7.5). Perl Speaks NONMEM (PsN, Version 5.3) was also used for facilitation of NONMEM tasks, such as VPC. Dataset preparation, data exploration, figures and tables, and derivation of posterior Bayes individual exposure levels were performed using R® V4.2 (or higher) with comprehensive R archive network (CRAN) and Certara packages.	Acceptable

L1564: **Population PK Model:** The final population PK model of inavolisib was a three-compartment model with first-order absorption, linear elimination, and a mixed proportional and additive error model. Effects of eGFR on CL, body weight and race on Vc and formulation on Ka were included. The parameter estimates for the final population PK model are presented in Table 48.

L1586: **Reviewer's comments:** The population PK modeling analyses for inavolisib are deemed acceptable. The reviewer was able to repeat and verify the Applicant's analyses with no significant discrepancies identified. Overall, the final population PK models appeared adequate for characterizing the PK profile of inavolisib both as a single agent and in combination with
L1587: *palbociclib and fulvestrant in patients with PIK3CA-mutated, HR-positive, HER2- negative locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer, as indicated in the applicant's goodness-of-fit plots and visual predictive check (VPC) plots. The results of the population PK analyses were used to support the proposed labeling statements regarding PK parameters of inavolisib and the effects of intrinsic and extrinsic factors.*
L1588: *However, there is insufficient information to assess the effect of moderate to severe hepatic impairment on inavolisib PK as no patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment were included in the population PK analyses. Similarly, the information to assess the effect of severe renal impairment (n=0) on inavolisib PK is absent. The covariate analyses suggested a significant increase (43%) in AUC is observed in 27 patients with moderate renal impairment. Refer to Section 6.1.2.2 for results of the moderate renal impairment group in the ongoing renal impairment study. Given the positive ER for safety, intolerable toxicity of 12 mg PO QD as monotherapy, and high rates of AEs observed in clinical studies at the proposed dosage, dedicated studies to accurately assess the impact of severe renal impairment and moderate to severe hepatic impairment on inavolisib PK are warranted.*
L1589: ## 19.4.3. Exposure-Response Analysis
L1590: ## 19.4.3.1. Executive Summary
L1591: The FDA's Assessment: The Applicant submitted one exposure-response (E-R) analysis report entitled "Exposure-Response Analyses of Inavolisib in Patients with PIK3CA-Mutant Solid Tumors and in Patients with PIK3CA-Mutant, HR positive, HER2-Negative Locally Advanced or Metastatic Breast Cancer".
L1592: The E-R analyses were conducted separately for the Phase 1 study GO39374 and the Phase 3 study WO41554. The E-R analyses were conducted to characterize the relationships between exposures of inavolisib and key efficacy and safety endpoints to support the proposed dosage used in the pivotal studies and in the label.
L1593: According to the E-R efficacy analysis for study GO39374, no exposure-response relationship was observed for ORR and BOR based on logistic regression models, and for PFS and DOR based on Cox models; lower and higher exposures of inavolisib achieved in Study GO39374 (dose range 3 to 12 mg with a large majority of patients receiving 9 mg) were associated with similar probabilities of response. In addition, multiple regimens (inavolisib monotherapy, doublet combinations, and triplet combinations) were included in this analysis.
L1594: According to the E-R efficacy analysis for study WO41554, no exposure-response relationship was observed for ORR, BOR, and CBR based on logistic regression models, and for PFS, DOR, and TFST based on Cox models; lower and higher exposures of inavolisib achieved at the clinical
L1595: dose (9 mg PO QD) were associated with similar effects. These data should be interpreted with caution given that only one inavolisib dose level was evaluated.
L1596: Based on exposure-safety analysis for study GO39374 using logistic regression analysis, positive exposure-response relationships were observed for the probability of grade \ge 3 AE, grade \ge 2 hyperglycemia, and inavolisib dose modification due to AE increased when AUC or C_{max} were used as exposure metrics.
L1597: Based on exposure-safety analysis for study WO41554 using logistic regression analysis, no exposure-response relationship was observed for most adverse events of interest (grade \ge 3 AE, grade \ge 2 neutropenia, grade \ge 2 hyperglycemia, grade \ge 2 thrombocytopenia, inavolisib dose modification due to AE, grade \ge 2 diarrhea, and grade \ge 2 stomatitis). However, the probability of grade \ge 2 anemia was higher with increasing inavolisib C_{max} values. Similar to the E-R efficacy analysis, these results should be interpreted with caution given that only one inavolisib dose level was evaluated.
L1598: The Applicant's analyses were repeated and deemed acceptable.
L1599: The Applicant's E-R analyses are summarized in detail below.
L1600: ## 19.4.3.2. E-R (efficacy) Assessment Summary
L1601: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1602: - Based on the exposure-efficacy analysis on a total of 149 patients with model-predicted exposure parameters in the inavolisib arm from Study WO41554, PFS, ORR, BOR, DOR, CBR, and time to TFST did not show an association with inavolisib exposure, after adjustment to baseline covariates (including baseline neutrophil count, AST, liver metastasis, and body surface area). Although similar OS was seen across C_{min} quartiles 1 - 3, numerically shorter OS was observed for quartile 4. Given that OS data could be confounded by the treatments received after study treatment discontinuation, and that OS data was not yet mature, TFST was investigated as another measure of long-term outcome. As outlined above, exposure-efficacy relationship was not significant for TFST.
- Based on the exposure-efficacy analysis on a total of 183 patients (Arm A to F) with model-predicted exposure parameters from the supportive study GO39374 (Dose range: 3, 6, 9, and 12 mg, with limited data from 3, 6, and 12 mg), ORR, BOR, DOR and PFS were not associated with inavolisib exposure, after adjustment to baseline covariates. However, these results should be interpreted with caution given that the data was pooled across multiple arms of Study GO39374 which include different combination partners across these arms.

Final Model Parameters	Summary	Acceptability [FDA's comments]
Model Structure	The relationships between efficacy and inavolisib exposure were explored using Kaplan-Meier curves and Cox proportional hazard models for the survival endpoints (PFS, OS, DOR, TFST), and logistic regressions for the binary endpoints (ORR, BOR, CBR). The Firth correction was implemented in the logistic regression when required to reduce the bias of maximum /likelihood estimates where small samples with unbalanced risk factors lead to separation problem	Acceptable
Model Parameter Estimates	
Phase III Study WO41554:
No exposure-response relationship was observed for ORR, BOR, and CBR based on logistic regression models and for PFS, DOR, and TFST based on Cox models after adjustment to baseline covariates. For OS, the upper quartile of Cmin (Q4) showed numerically shorter survival than other quartiles despite adjustment to baseline covariates while similar OS was seen across the three other quartiles. See Tables 21, 23, 25, 26-29 in Report 1129439.
Phase I Study GO39374:
No exposure-response relationship was observed for ORR, BOR based on logistic regression models and for PFS and DOR based on Cox models. (see Tables 6, 8, 9, 10 in Report 1129439).
Acceptable
Model Evaluation	Graphical exploration and model evaluation by change of objective function and statistical significance of model parameters (stepwise forward inclusion and backward elimination). First, a stepwise forward addition test was performed on relevant covariates, keeping in the model for the statistically significant covariate that reduce AIC the most, one-by-one, until no further covariate could be included. The statistical significance of model parameters was tested in the logistic regression models with a p-value of 0.05. Each exposure metric (AUC, Cmin, Cmax) was tested one by one as the last step of forward addition, and the exposure parameter that reduced the AIC the most (if any), while being statistically significant, was added to the model. The model at this stage was referred to as full model. Next, the full model was inspected where the categorized continuous covariates or the categorical covariates with p-value above 0.1 were lumped with the default category, and the resulting model was re-estimated. Categories with skip ordinal won't be combined (i.e., not combining quartile 1 with quartile 3, or normal renal function with moderate impairment). The model at this stage was referred to as simplified full model. Finally, a stepwise backward elimination was performed by removing covariates/exposure parameter from the simplified full model one at a time, if their deletion leads to insignificant model deterioration. The most insignificant covariate was removed first, and the procedure was repeated until no further insignificant covariate relationship was detected. Significance level of 0.01 was used in the backward elimination. The change of OFV has accounted for the change of degree of freedom, i.e., 6.635, 9.21, and 11.34 points for one, two, three degrees of freedoms, respectively. The model obtained was called the final model.	Acceptable
Covariates and Clinical Relevance	
Phase III Study WO41554:
Neutrophil count at baseline as quartile was identified as covariates consistently in the final model of CBR, PFS, OS, and TFST, with Q4 being statistically significant (worse response); Other covariates, such as Aspartate Aminotransferase, liver metastasis and BSA were identified in certain efficacy endpoints. Exposure metrics (Cmin) was only identified as covariate for OS, with Q4 showing worse OS. Given that OS data could be impacted by treatments received after study treatment discontinuation, and that OS data was not yet mature, TFST was investigated as another measure of longer-term outcome, there was no exposure-response relationship for this endpoint.
The analyses outcomes indicated that patients benefited similarly across the range of exposures following 9 mg QD.
Phase I Study GO39374:
Acceptable

L1609: {Inavolisib}
L1610: For the dose range tested (3, 6, 9, and 12 mg, with limited data from 3, 6, and 12 mg), ORR, BOR, DOR and PFS were not associated with inavolisib exposure, after adjustment to baseline covariates. Measurable disease was identified as covariate in the final model of ORR and BOR (the presence of measurable disease was associated with a higher probability of response); Combination status (Triplet), Liver Metastasis and Baseline Tumor Burden were identified as covariates in the final model of PFS (liver metastasis and higher baseline tumor burden led to shorter PFS, and triplet combination was associated with longer PFS).
The analyses outcome indicated better efficacy in Triplet combination (with a CDK4/6 inhibitor and endocrine agent), and worse efficacy for higher tumor burden and Liver Metastasis.

L1612: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219249}
## {Inavolisib}
L1613: Model Parameter Estimates	
Phase III Study WO41554: No exposure-response relationships (both probability and time to onset) were observed for Grade \ge 3 AE, Neutropenia Grade \ge 2, Hyperglycemia Grade \ge 2, Thrombocytopenia \ge 2, inavolisib dose Modification due to AE, Diarrhea Grade \ge 2, Stomatitis Grade \ge 2; positive exposure-response relationship was observed for probability of Anemia Grade \ge 2 (see Tables 31-48 in Report 1129439).
Phase I Study GO39374: Positive exposure-response relationships (both probability and time to onset) were observed for Grade \ge 3 AE, hyperglycemia Grade \ge 2, and inavolisib dose modification due to AE (see Tables 12-16 in Report 1129439).

Model Evaluation	Same as exposure-efficacy analysis	
Covariates and Clinical Relevance	
Phase III Study WO41554: Various baseline covariates were identified for the safety endpoints, such as Hemoglobin for Grade \ge 2 anemia (probability and time to onset), Neutrophil count for Grade \ge 2 Neutropenia (probability and time to onset), and Platelet count for Grade \ge 2 Thrombocytopenia (probability and time to onset). The identification of those covariates in the model were expected because as lab values they are highly relevant to the respective AE endpoints. Race was also identified as covariate for Grade \ge 2 Hyperglycemia and Thrombocytopenia (probability and time to onset), and for Grade \ge 2 anemia and Grade \ge 3 AE (time to onset), with higher probability or early on-set of those AEs in Asian patients, however, the rate of inavo dose modification or dose reduction in Asian or Chinese patients are comparable to those in the global patients.
Phase I Study GO39374: Patients with high risk factors for Hyperglycemia at Study Entry (i.e., BMI \ge 30 or HbA1C \ge 5.7\%) are at a higher probability of experiencing an AE Grade \ge 3 or hyperglycemia Grade \ge 2; this was expected given hyperglycemia is an on-target AE for inavolisib. Patients receiving inavolisib in combination with palbociclib and fulvestrant are also at a higher probability of experiencing an AE Grade \ge 3; this was expected given neutropenia is the most common AE for palbociclib.

Simulation for Specific Population	Not applicable	
Visualization of E-R relationships	The relationship between inavolisib exposure metric and the AE endpoints (time to onset and probability) were graphically explored using Kaplan-Meier plots and logistic plots.	
Overall Clinical Relevance for ER	Although positive relationship between inavolisib exposure and safety endpoints (Grade \ge 3 AE, hyperglycemia Grade \ge 2, and inavolisib dose modification) were revealed in Study GO39374, no positive relationship was found for WO41554 (except Anemia Grade \ge 2). This can be explained by a wider exposure range in study GO39374 compared to Study WO41554, broader HbA1c and fasting blood glucose eligibility criteria in Study GO39374 compared to Study WO41554, and better AE management in WO41554.

L1619: ## 19.4.3.3. E-R (safety) Assessment Summary
L1620: **The Applicant's Position:** Exposure-safety analyses were performed based on a total of 165 patients with model-predicted exposure parameters from Study GO39374 (Arm F was not part of the safety analysis because anti-hyperglycemic medication was given to all patients in Arm F from Day 1).
L1621: Based on exposure-safety analysis of adverse events of interest using logistic regression models, the probability of AE Grade \ge 3, hyperglycemia Grade \ge 2, and inavolisib dose modification due to AE increased as a function of exposure parameter AUC or Cmax. In addition, time to first instance of AE Grade \ge 3, hyperglycemia Grade \ge 2, and inavolisib dose modification due to AE was shorter in patients with higher inavolisib AUC.
L1622: Exposure-safety analyses were performed based on a total of 149 patients with model-predicted exposure parameters in the inavolisib arm from Study WO41554. No exposure-response relationship was observed for most adverse events of interest (grade \ge 3 AE, neutropenia Grade \ge 2, hyperglycemia Grade \ge 2, thrombocytopenia Grade \ge 2, inavolisib dose modification due to AE, diarrhea Grade \ge 2, stomatitis Grade \ge 2) based on logistic regression models; lower and higher exposures of inavolisib achieved at the clinical dose (9 mg PO QD) were associated with similar safety outcomes. The probability of anemia Grade \ge 2 was higher with increasing inavolisib Cmax values while there was no relationship between time to onset of anemia and inavolisib exposure.

L1644: ## 19.4.3.4. Overall benefit-risk evaluation based on E-R analyses
L1645: **The Applicant's Position:** Exposure-efficacy analyses supported 9 mg QD inavolisib as an efficacious dose in the context of the positive topline efficacy results for treatment (Inavo+Palbo+Fulv) versus reference (Pbo+Palbo+Fulv) groups.
L1646: Inavolisib exposure was not significantly associated with ORR, BOR, DOR, CBR, PFS, or TFST based on Study WO41554, after adjustment to baseline covariates, indicating that patients benefited similarly across the range of observed exposures following 9 mg QD. For OS, exposures of inavolisib achieved at the 9 mg QD were associated with similar effect on OS with the exception of the upper quartile of Cmin (Q4) which showed a lower probability of survival. This should be interpreted with caution; indeed, OS was not mature yet and could have been impacted by treatments after study treatment discontinuation.
L1647: In Study WO41554, higher inavolisib exposure was associated with a higher risk for Grade \ge 2 anemia, but not with any of the other AE of interest (i.e., Grade \ge 2 hyperglycemia, neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, diarrhea, stomatitis; any Grade \ge 3 AE; inavolisib dose modification due to AE), after adjustment to baseline covariates.
L1648: The 9 mg QD dose of inavolisib demonstrated high median relative dose intensity of
L1649: inavolisib. Patients with exposure metrics in Q4 were associated with a lower dose intensity, most likely due to dose reduction/dose hold. However, the median value of dose intensity across exposure cohorts were all above or equal to 90%, indicating good tolerability across the exposure range for patients who were administered the 9 mg QD dose.
L1650: **The FDA's Assessment:** FDA agrees that inavolisib 9 mg QD is an effective dosage based on improved PFS for inavolisib + palbociclib + fulvestrant compared to placebo + palbociclib + fulvestrant in patients with HR+, HER2- locally advanced or metastatic breast cancer. However, FDA disagrees that 9 mg QD is an optimized dosage for the targeted indication. See details in section 6.3.2.2.
L1651: ## 19.4.4. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic Modeling Review
L1652: ## 19.4.4.1. Executive Summary
L1653: **The FDA's Assessment:** The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant's physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) analyses to evaluate the drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of inavolisib as a precipitant of the CYP3A substrate midazolam.
L1654: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report (1129287) and related model summary reports, response to FDA information request dated 15 July 2024 (seq0027), and the modeling supporting files, and concluded that PBPK analysis cannot rule out the induction potential of inavolisib on the sensitive CYP3A substrate midazolam. The magnitude of inavolisib induction on the exposure of substrates of CYP3A cannot be confidently predicted due to the uncertainties about the induction parameters and methodology.
L1655: ## 19.4.4.2. Methods
L1656: **The FDA's Assessment:** All simulations were performed using the PK/PD Profiles mode in the Simcyp® Simulator (Versions 22, Certara, Sheffield, UK) with the healthy subject population (sim-Healthy Volunteers) model. Age, sex, subject number, and dosing regimens were consistent with the actual trial design. Ten trials were simulated for each simulation scenario. Schemes of the PBPK modeling and simulation strategy are shown in Figure 16, which summarizes the studies used for inavolisib model development and verification, and model applications in predicting DDI of inavolisib as a precipitant of CYP3A4 induction or inhibition. The final model input parameters were summarized in Table 50. The inavolisib PBPK model consists of an Advanced Dissolution, Absorption and Metabolism (ADAM) model for describing drug absorption in each gut segment, a full PBPK model (method 3) for distribution, and an enzyme kinetics model and renal clearance for elimination. The Simcyp library models of midazolam, triazolam, itraconazole, rifampin and erythromycin were used without any modification, except for CYP3A4 TDI parameters for erythromycin (see Results, section 2).
L1657: {Inavolisib}
L1658: A published palbociclib PBPK model (PMID: 27402157) was used to verify the inavolisib interaction parameters. At the reviewer's request (FDA Information Request dated 15 July 2024), the Applicant evaluated the performance of this model using Simcyp version 22, and the results are shown in itraconazole and **Table 51** Predicted and observed palbociclib exposure in the presence and absence of itraconazole and rifampin. In general, the model could capture the effects of itraconazole and rifampin on palbociclib exposure, but it tended to underpredict the C_{max} and AUC_{inf} of palbociclib, especially in the itraconazole study. Thus, the reviewer modified the Applicant's model by increasing the fraction absorbed (fa) from 0.85 to 0.975 and reducing the additional human liver microsome clearance from 37.65 to 20 mL/min/mg protein. As a result, the fraction metabolized by CYP3A4 was changed from 53% to 62%. The performance of this modified model was evaluated and results are summarized in **Figure 17**, **Figure 18** and **Table 51**.

L1677: ## 19.4.4.3. Results
L1678: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1679: ## 1. Can the PBPK model adequately describe the PK profiles of inavolisib?
L1680: Yes. The model could reasonably well describe the plasma concentration-time profiles of inavolisib following single and multiple oral doses of inavolisib in patients with cancer, and the model-estimated AUC and Cmax were mostly within 0.8- and 1.25-fold of the observed values. Simulated and observed inavolisib PK profiles and parameters are summarized in Figure 19 Figure 20 and Table 52.

L1691: ## 2. Can PBPK analyses be used to estimate the effects of inavolisib on the CYP3A substrate midazolam?
L1692: The potential induction effect of inavolisib on the sensitive CYP3A substrate midazolam following oral administration of inavolisib 9-mg QD cannot be ruled out for the reasons discussed below.
L1693: In vitro, inavolisib is a time-dependent inhibitor and inducer of CYP3A. Following oral administration of single and multiple doses of inavolisib, inavolisib AUC increased approximately proportional to the dose. The clinical DDI study of inavolisib with midazolam in patients with cancer has not been conducted. The available clinical PK data are insufficient to differentiate the CYP3A inhibition or induction of inavolisib, thus the CYP3A inhibition and induction parameters of inavolisib cannot be independently verified. In addition, the ability of PBPK modeling to accurately predict complex DDI has not been demonstrated. A study conducted by the Innovation and Quality (IQ) Induction Working Group showed that, for compounds that were both in vitro time-dependent inhibitors and inducers of CYP3A, prediction of the DDI trended toward an overprediction of the inactivation or a net effect of no interaction (PMID: 23995268). The inhibition or induction effects of this type of compound should be evaluated separately.
L1694: ## Inhibitory Effect of inavolisib on CYP3A
L1695: The time-dependent inhibition studies were conducted in human liver microsomes (HLM) using testosterone and midazolam as probe CYP3A substrates and in primary human hepatocytes using midazolam as a substrate (Study 16-0806). The Applicant used the kinact and Ki values estimated from the HLM data in the inavolisib model for predicting its inhibitory effect on midazolam. In the HLM study, the kinact and Ki values could only be estimated from the data generated using midazolam, while values could not be determined using testosterone because
L1696: the maximum inactivation rate was not achieved under the experimental condition. In this study, erythromycin was used as a positive control and its k_{\text{inact}} and K_I values were also generated using midazolam as a substrate. To examine the ability of the in vitro TDI parameters of inavolisib to predict DDI, the k_{\text{inact}} and K_I values in the SV-erythromycin model was replaced with the one generated by the Applicant, and simulations of clinical DDIs of erythromycin with midazolam or triazolam were performed. The effects of erythromycin were reasonably predicted (Table 53), which provides some confidence in predicting CYP3A inhibition using parameters generated under the same experimental condition. When simulating the DDI effect of inavolisib on midazolam using only the k_{\text{inact}} and K_I values from the HLM study, inavolisib was predicted to increase midazolam AUC and C_{\text{max}} by 17% and 10%, respectively. This result suggests that inavolisib is expected to have a minimal inhibitory effect on midazolam exposure. Therefore, the effect of inavolisib on midazolam exposure, if any, could be mainly driven by the induction effect of inavolisib.
L1697: Of note, in the IC50 shift assay, greater shift and lower IC50 were observed when testosterone was used as a CYP3A substrate, suggesting that testosterone may be more sensitive to inavolisib inhibition. If this is the case, the inhibitory effect of inavolisib on CYP3A may potentially be underestimated.

L1702: ## Induction effect of inavolisib on CYP3A
L1703: Inavolisib increased mRNA and activity of CYP3A in hepatocytes from three donors (namely, FOS, 1198 and 1421, report 16-0131). The induction parameters of inavolisib were estimated based on the response of mRNA expression in these hepatocytes to inavolisib, and then calibrated to the induction parameters of rifampin generated from the same hepatocytes (Table 54). Using the calibrated parameters, inavolisib was predicted to have a weak induction effect on CYP3A based on the parameters generated from lot FOS (Table 54). However, there is uncertainty about using the calibrated parameters to estimate induction effect. Although the induction calibration method was published by Simcyp (PMID: 27026679), in the Simcyp PBPK models of CYP3A inducers except for phenobarbital, CYP3A induction parameters optimized with clinical DDI data were used instead of calibrated parameters published in the aforementioned paper, indicating that clinical DDI data were needed to adequately predict the induction effects of CYP3A inducers.
L1704: In the clinical trial GO39374, palbociclib was co-administered with 9-mg QD inavolisib along with letrozole. The steady state PK parameters of palbociclib in this study were mostly within 30% of the values published in the Palbociclib FDA Clinical Pharmacology Review (NDA 207103 Review, 2014). During inavolisib model verification, the Applicant simulated palbociclib PK in this study using the induction parameters generated from lot 1198 together with CYP3A TDI parameters generated in human liver microsomes. The ability of the simulations to capture the steady state palbociclib PK profile was used as the evidence that the CYP3A interaction parameters were adequately established (Figure 21). The reviewer performed additional simulations to examine the effects of inavolisib on palbociclib PK when inavolisib behaved as a CYP3A inducer. These simulations were performed using the Applicant's palbociclib model or the reviewer-modified model (see Methods) and the induction parameters generated from lot FOS but without the CYP3A TDI parameters. The results showed that the steady-state palbociclib PK profiles and parameters were not significantly changed (Figure 21 and Table 55) even when inavolisib was predicted to reduce midazolam AUC close to 50% under these conditions (Table 56). Therefore, capturing the steady state palbociclib PK profiles is not sufficient evidence to verify the CYP3A interaction parameters.
L1705: Of note, although cytotoxicity was not observed in hepatocytes from lot 1198 and lot 1421, crystallization was observed from cell morphology at 100 \muM inavolisib, which may be the potential reason for the reduced response observed at 100 \muM compared to 10 \muM, the second highest concentration (Report 16-0131). The maximum induction responses of the hepatocytes from lot 1198 and lot 1421 to inavolisib were not reached, which may affect the accuracy of induction parameter estimation.
L1706: Taken together, the effect of inavolisib on CYP3A substrates cannot be confidently predicted. The potential induction effect of inavolisib on CYP3A cannot be ruled out.

L1723: ## 19.4.4.4. Conclusion
L1724: **The FDA's Assessment:** PBPK analysis cannot rule out the induction potential of inavolisib on the sensitive CYP3A substrate midazolam. The magnitude of inavolisib induction on the exposure of substrates of CYP3A cannot be confidently predicted due to the uncertainties about the induction parameters and methodology.

L1766: Model Development
System model
ADAM absorption model
Full body distribution model
Well-stirred hepatic model with enzyme kinetics
Simple CL_r input for renal elimination
Drug model
Physicochemical properties based on in vitro measurements
K_{in} scalar, CL_r, hepatic CL_{int} all based on mass balance study
Enzyme inhibition and induction parameters based on experiments
Model confirmation comparing simulated inavolisib PK profile and exposure metrics after IV and PO dosing with observed data from mass balance study
Observed dataset:
Inavolisib IV 0.1 mg single dose
Inavolisib PO 9 mg single dose
Model Validation
Model validation comparing simulated inavolisib PK profile after single and multiple dosing with observed data from Phase 1a monotherapy cohort
Observed dataset:
Inavolisib PO 6 mg, 9 mg, and 12 mg multiple dosing
Model validation comparing simulated palbociclib and inavolisib PK profile after single and multiple dosing with observed data from Phase 1b combination cohort
Observed dataset:
Inavolisib PO 9 mg multiple dosing
Palbociclib PO 125 mg multiple dosing
Model Application
Prediction of CYP3A4-mediated DDI between inavolisib 9 mg and midazolam:
Simulation of AUC and C_{max} of midazolam in the presence and absence of inavolisib to calculate GMR
Scenario 1: CYP3A4 TDI only
Scenario 2: CYP3A4 induction only, using all three different hepatocyte lots/donors
Scenario 3: CYP3A4 TDI + induction, using all three different hepatocyte lots/donors
L1767: Figure 17 displays two graphs comparing observed and simulated plasma concentration-time profiles of palbociclib after a single 125-mg oral dose, with and without coadministration of itraconazole (Itraconazole DDI).
The Y-axis represents Systemic Concentration (ng/mL) on a logarithmic scale (0 to 100). The X-axis represents Time - Substrate (h) (96 to 264).
The left graph is labeled "Itraconazole DDI" and the right graph is labeled "Itraconazole DDI (reviewer's model)".
In both graphs, white circles represent observed palbociclib PK data following a single dose of 125 mg of palbociclib on Day 5 in the absence of itraconazole, and gray circles represent observed data in the presence of itraconazole (200 mg QD orally from Day 1 to Day 11).
Solid lines represent the simulated palbociclib profiles in the absence of itraconazole, and dashed lines represent the profiles in the presence of itraconazole. Black lines represent the simulation mean, and gray lines represent the 5th and 95th percentiles of the simulations.
The presence of itraconazole significantly increases the systemic concentration of palbociclib, particularly during the initial phase (up to 120 hours).
L1768: Figure 18 displays four graphs comparing observed and simulated plasma concentration-time profiles of palbociclib following a single dose, either oral (PO) or intravenous (IV) infusion, across semi-log and linear scales.
The Y-axis represents Systemic Concentration (ng/mL). The X-axis represents Time (h) (0 to 144).
The top row shows results for Palbociclib 50 mg IV Infusion, and the bottom row shows results for Palbociclib 125 mg PO.
The left column uses a semi-log scale for concentration, and the right column uses a linear scale for concentration.
In all four graphs, white circles represent observed palbociclib PK data, and solid lines represent the simulated profiles. Gray lines represent the 5th and 95th percentiles of the simulations.
The IV infusion profiles show rapid initial decline, while the PO profiles show a peak concentration around 24 hours.
